Conformal structures with G 2 G_{2} -symmetric twistor distribution
Pawel Nurowski, Katja Sagerschnig, Dennis The

Abstract
. For any 4D split-signature conformal structure, there is an induced twistor distribution on the 5D space of all self-dual totally null 2-planes, which is ( 2 , 3 , 5 ) (2,3,5) when the conformal structure is not anti-self-dual. Several examples where the twistor distribution achieves maximal symmetry (the split-real form of the exceptional simple Lie algebra of type G 2 \mathrm{G}_{2} ) were previously known, and these include fascinating examples arising from the rolling of surfaces without twisting or slipping. Relaxing the rolling assumption, we establish a complete local classification result among those homogeneous 4D split-conformal structures for which the symmetry algebra induces a multiply-transitive action on the 5D space. Furthermore, we discuss geometric properties of these conformal structures such as their curvature, holonomy, and existence of Einstein representatives.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 53C30, 53Z05

1.
Introduction

One of the unexpected appearances of the split-real form
{\mathfrak{g}}
of the exceptional simple Lie algebra of type
\mathrm{G}_{2}
is the following. Consider a ball with radius
r
rolling on another ball with radius
R
without slipping or twisting. Then the configuration space is a
5
-manifold
N
, and a curve represents ‘rolling without slipping or twisting’ if and only if it is everywhere tangent to a certain rank two distribution
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}_{r:R}\subset TN
– the
rolling distribution
of the two balls. Provided that
r\neq R
,
{\mathcal{D}}
is a
(2,3,5)
-distribution, i.e.
[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]
has constant rank
3
and
[{\mathcal{D}},[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]]=TN
. The distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
always has a natural
\mathfrak{so}(3)\times\mathfrak{so}(3)
-symmetry, but it turns out that the Lie algebra of (infinitesimal) symmetries of
{\mathcal{D}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathsf{Lie}(\mathrm{G}_{2})
if and only if the ratio of the radii of the two balls is
1:3
or
3:1
, see
[
1
,
2
,
5
,
8
,
25
,
27
]
.

In
[
2
]
, the authors asked whether there are
other pairs of surfaces whose rolling distribution has symmetry
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathsf{Lie}(\mathrm{G}_{2})
. In the course of their investigations, they identified the rolling distribution as a special case of the following more general construction. Consider a
4
-manifold
M^{4}
equipped with an
oriented split-signature conformal structure

[\mathsf{g}]
. It has two families of totally null
2
-planes: self-dual (SD) and anti-self-dual (ASD) ones. In particular, one can form the circle bundle

(1.1)

\mathbf{S}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}^{+}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}M^{4}

of all SD totally null
2
-planes. The total space
N^{5}={\mathbb{T}}^{+}
is 5-dimensional and carries a natural rank two distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, called the
twistor distribution
, see Section
2.2
for the definition.
1
1

1

There is an analogous twistor distribution on the ASD side, but the majority of our article uses the SD construction, so we have chosen to not emphasize this via augmented notation, e.g.
(\ell^{+},{\mathcal{D}}^{+})
. Generally, SD will be implicit, but we will emphasize when we consider the ASD construction instead.
It is
(2,3,5)
on open subsets in
\mathbb{T}^{+}
provided the SD part
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
of the Weyl tensor of
[\mathsf{g}]
is
non-vanishing
. Moreover, given two Riemannian surfaces
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{g}_{1})
and
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{g}_{2})
, the twistor distribution of the conformal manifold
(\Sigma_{1}\times\Sigma_{2},[\mathsf{g}_{1}-\mathsf{g}_{2}])
can be naturally identified with the rolling distribution of the two surfaces.

This leads to the more general question
[
2
]
:
For which
4D
split-conformal structures does the twistor distribution have (infinitesimal)
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetry?
Several of such structures are already known. One example is represented by Brinkmann’s Ricci-flat pp-wave metric
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{d}x\mathsf{d}u+\mathsf{d}y\mathsf{d}v+x^{2}\mathsf{d}v^{2}
originating in
[
10
]
, for which
[\mathsf{g}]
is submaximally symmetric and has 9-dimensional symmetry
[
14
,
19
]
. (In 4D, the maximum conformal symmetry dimension is 15.) Two more examples come from the
\mathfrak{sl}(3,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant
dancing metric
and its
\mathfrak{su}(1,2)
-invariant analogue
[
14
,
9
,
20
]
.

The aforementioned rolling distribution of two balls with ratio of radii
1:3
comes from a conformal structure with 6-dimensional symmetry.
Beyond this rolling example, three more conformal structures of this sort where found in
[
2
]
. They correspond to surfaces of revolution
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{g}_{1})
rolling on the flat plane
(\Sigma_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{2},\mathsf{g}_{2}=\mathsf{d}x^{2}+\mathsf{d}y^{2})
. Their conformal structures
[\mathsf{g}]
are represented by
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{g}_{1}-\mathsf{g}_{2}
, with

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{1}=(\rho^{2}+\epsilon)^{2}\mathsf{d}\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}\mathsf{d}\varphi^{2},\qquad\epsilon\in\{0,\pm 1\},

and their twistor/rolling distributions have
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetry. Concerning conformal symmetries of
[\mathsf{g}]
, it admits the symmetries
\partial_{\varphi},\partial_{x},\partial_{y},-y\partial_{x}+x\partial_{y}
. When
\epsilon=\pm 1
, this is the entire conformal symmetry algebra of
[\mathsf{g}]
, and the conformal structure is
non-homogeneous
. When
\epsilon=0
, these symmetries are augmented by the scaling symmetry
\rho\partial_{\rho}+2\varphi\partial_{\phi}+3x\partial_{x}+3y\partial_{y}
, making the
\epsilon=0
structure
homogeneous
.

In our present article, we begin a systematic classification programme of
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric twistor distributions. We concentrate on the conformally homogeneous setting.

In order to formulate our main result, let us make the following key observations: First, the vertical bundle
\ell=\ker(\pi_{*})
of (
1.1
) is distinguished. This leads us to consider on
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
the enhanced structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
, which we call the
twistor XXO-structure of
[\mathsf{g}]
. (The “XXO” name comes from the marked Dynkin diagram encoding of the geometry.) Diagrammatically, we have the following:

twistor XXO-structure

(N^{5}={\mathbb{T}}^{+};\ell,{\mathcal{D}})

(2,3,5)
-distribution

(U^{5};{\mathcal{D}})

(oriented) split-conformal

structure
(M^{4};[\mathsf{g}])

\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]\neq{\mathcal{D}}
on
\scriptstyle U\subset N^{5}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Moreover, infinitesimal conformal symmetries, i.e. conformal Killing fields of
(M^{4},[\mathsf{g}])
, lift to to symmetries of the twistor XXO-structure, i.e. to vector fields on
N^{5}
that via the Lie derivative preserve the pair of distributions
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
. Indeed the lift defines an isomorphism between the symmetry algebra of the conformal structure and that of the twistor XXO-structure.

From now on, we will restrict to conformal structures with
multiply-transitive
twistor XXO-structures, i.e. we assume that the symmetry algebra
{\mathfrak{f}}
of the XXO-structure acts transitively with isotropy algebra
{\mathfrak{f}}^{0}
(at a generic point) having
{\rm dim}({\mathfrak{f}}^{0})\geq 1
. Thus, we are focusing on homogeneous structures with
{\rm dim}({\mathfrak{f}})\geq 6
. (In particular, the examples (
1.2
) lie outside the scope of our present investigations.) Our main result is:

Theorem 1.1
.

The complete local classification of
4D
split-conformal structures with multiply-transitive twistor XXO-structure and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric twistor distribution is given in Table
1
.

\begin{array}[]{|l|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\mbox{Label}&\mbox{Representative metric $\mathsf{g}$}&\mbox{Petrov type }&\begin{tabular}[]{c}Conformal\\

Table 1.
All
4D
split-conformal structures
(M^{4};[\mathsf{g}])
with multiply-transitive twistor XXO-structure
(N^{5};\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric twistor distribution
(N^{5};{\mathcal{D}})

Equally important in our story are the techniques used to derive our main result above, and alternative manners of presenting it. For any homogeneous structure, we emphasize three
equivalent
ways of presenting it: (i) in local coordinates, (ii) Lie-theoretic, and (iii) Cartan-theoretic. The Petrov types listed above concern the root types of the SD part
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
and ASD part
{\mathcal{W}}^{-}
of the Weyl tensor, viewed as a binary quartic in spinorial language. We circumvent working directly with spinorial Weyl tensor coefficients, and instead demonstrate how these Petrov types are efficiently obtained via (SD & ASD) twistor XXO-structures starting from each of the presentations (i)–(iii) above.

The Cartan-theoretic presentation is the most abstract, but has the advantage of giving us a clear understanding of the curvature of these geometries, and an easy way to compute their (conformal) holonomy (Table
15
). In turn, this allows us to efficiently
algebraically
assess which of the conformal structures
[\mathsf{g}]
admit Einstein representatives on an open subset (Theorem
5.9
).

2.
Geometric structures

2.1.
(2,3,5)
-distributions

The study of
(2,3,5)
-distributions has a long history, originating in the seminal 1910 work of Élie Cartan
[
13
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
(2,3,5)
-distribution

{\mathcal{D}}
on a
5
-manifold
N
is a rank 2 subbundle
{\mathcal{D}}\subset TN
such that
[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]
is a rank
3
subbundle of
TN
and
[{\mathcal{D}},[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]]=TN
.

Any
(2,3,5)
-distribution can be locally specified in terms of a function
F=F(x,y,z,p,q)
satisfying
F_{qq}\neq 0
. Namely, there exist local coordinates
(x,y,p,q,z)
such that the distribution is the common kernel of the
1
-forms

(2.1)

\mathsf{d}y-p\mathsf{d}x,\quad\mathsf{d}p-q\mathsf{d}x,\quad\mathsf{d}z-F\mathsf{d}x,

or equivalently, it is spanned by the vector fields

\partial_{q}

and
\partial_{x}+p\partial_{y}+q\partial_{p}+F\partial_{z}.

Infinitesimal symmetries of
{\mathcal{D}}
are vector fields
X\in\Gamma(TN)
such that
\mathcal{L}_{X}Y=[X,Y]\in\Gamma({\mathcal{D}})
for all
Y\in\Gamma({\mathcal{D}})
. A local model of the maximally symmetric
(2,3,5)
-distribution whose Lie algebra of infinitesimal symmetries is the split-real form of the
14
-dimensional exceptional simple Lie algebra of type
\mathrm{G}_{2}
can, for example, be obtained by choosing
F=q^{2}
in (
2.1
).

2.2.
Geometry on the circle twistor bundle

In
[
2
]
, the construction of
(2,3,5)
-distributions from rolling surfaces was interpreted as a special case of a construction familiar from twistor theory.

Let
(M,[\mathsf{g}])
be a smooth, oriented, conformal
4
-manifold of signature
(2,2)
. Locally, one can always find a null coframe such that the metric
\mathsf{g}
and volume form
\mathrm{vol}_{\mathsf{g}}
are expressed as

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{g}_{ij}\theta^{i}\theta^{j}=2(\theta^{1}\theta^{2}+\theta^{3}\theta^{4}),\quad\mathrm{vol}_{\mathsf{g}}=\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{4},

where
\theta^{i}\theta^{j}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta^{i}\otimes\theta^{j}+\theta^{j}\otimes\theta^{i})
.
Then the Hodge-star operator
\star:\Lambda^{2}T^{*}M\to\Lambda^{2}T^{*}M
, defined via the relation
\alpha\wedge*\beta=\mathsf{g}(\alpha,\beta)\mathrm{vol}_{\mathsf{g}}
, satisfies

(2.3)

\displaystyle\star\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2}=-\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{4},\quad\star\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{3}=-\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{3},\quad\star\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{4}=\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{4},

\displaystyle\star\theta^{3}\wedge\theta^{4}=-\theta^{1}\wedge\theta^{2},\quad\star\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{4}=-\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{4},\quad\star\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3}=\theta^{2}\wedge\theta^{3}.

Let
\Pi_{x}\subset T_{x}M
be a totally null 2-plane and suppose that
\phi
and
\psi
span its annihilator. Then
\Pi
is
self-dual (SD)
(respectively,
anti-self-dual (ASD)
) if and only if
\phi\wedge\psi
is an eigenvector of the Hodge star operator with eigenvalue
+1
(respectively,
-1
).

The set of all totally null 2-planes in
T_{x}M
is a disjoint union of the SD and ASD ones, denoted
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}(T_{x}M)
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{-}(T_{x}M)
respectively, each of which is diffeomorphic to
\mathbf{S}^{1}
. Let us focus on SD family. (Considerations for the ASD family are similar.) One can form the
circle twistor bundle

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{1}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{T}}^{+}:=\bigsqcup_{x\in M}{\mathbb{T}}^{+}(T_{x}M)\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}M,

where
\pi
is the natural projection
(x,\Pi)\mapsto x
. The total space
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
is naturally equipped with the following geometric structures:

•

A rank one vertical bundle
\ell=\ker(\pi_{*})\subset T{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
.

•

A canonical rank three distribution
{\mathcal{H}}\subset T{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
: A point
p=(x,\Pi)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
corresponds to a SD totally null
2
-plane
\Pi\subset T_{x}M
and we define
{\mathcal{H}}_{p}:=\{X\in T_{p}{\mathbb{T}}^{+}\ |\ \pi_{*}X\in\Pi\}
.
This distribution is
maximally non-integrable
, i.e.
[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]=T{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
.

•

Maximal non-integrability means that the map
\Lambda^{2}{\mathcal{H}}\to T{\mathbb{T}}^{+}/{\mathcal{H}}
(induced by the Lie bracket) is surjective, so has a
1
-dimensional kernel. The kernel is spanned by decomposable elements and thus defines a rank two distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
uniquely characterized by the properties

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{H}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]\subset{\mathcal{H}}.

Definition 2.2
.

The canonical rank two distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
is called the (SD)
twistor distribution
. The structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
is called the (SD)
twistor XXO-structure
. We have
{\mathcal{H}}=\ell\oplus{\mathcal{D}}
.

A vector field
X
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{X}\Gamma({\mathcal{D}})\subset\Gamma({\mathcal{D}})
and
\mathcal{L}_{X}\Gamma(\ell)\subset\Gamma(\ell)
is called an
infinitesimal symmetry
of the XXO-structure. Conformal symmetries of
[\mathsf{g}]
naturally lift from
M
to
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
. Indeed, the lift defines an isomorphism between the symmetry algebras of the conformal structure
(M,[\mathsf{g}])
and that of the XXO-geometry on
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
. This follows most easily from the Cartan geometric description of the construction discussed in Section
3.4
. The dimension of the symmetry algebra of the twistor distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
alone is in general larger than that of the conformal structure.

To obtain the twistor distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
explicitly, let
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\theta^{3},\theta^{4})
be a positively oriented null (local) coframing on
M
as in (
2.2
) and let
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
be the dual (local) framing. The set of all SD totally null
2
-planes at a point
2
2

2

We note that the ASD totally null 2-planes are given by
\langle se_{1}+te_{4},se_{3}-te_{2}\rangle
.
can be parametrized as follows

(2.6)

\displaystyle\Pi(s,t)=\langle se_{1}+te_{3},se_{4}-te_{2}\rangle=\mathsf{ker}(s\theta^{2}+t\theta^{4},s\theta^{3}-t\theta^{1}),\quad[s:t]\in{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{P}}^{1}\cong{\mathbf{S}}^{1}.

Let us work in a local trivialization
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}|_{U}:=\pi^{-1}(U)\to U\times{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{P}}^{1}
. Restrict to the open subset where
s\neq 0
and introduce an affine (fibre) coordinate
\xi=\tfrac{t}{s}
.

Then
{\mathcal{H}}=\mathsf{ker}(\omega^{1},\omega^{2})
, with

(2.7)

\displaystyle\omega^{1}=\theta^{2}+\xi\theta^{4},\quad\omega^{2}=\theta^{3}-\xi\theta^{1},

where we have omitted pullbacks to simplify notation. Moreover,
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathsf{ker}(\omega^{1},\omega^{2},\omega^{3})
, where
\omega^{3}
is chosen such that
[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
. Equivalently, this means that

(2.8)

\mathsf{d}\omega^{1}\wedge\omega^{1}\wedge\omega^{2}\wedge\omega^{3}=0,\quad\mathsf{d}\omega^{2}\wedge\omega^{1}\wedge\omega^{2}\wedge\omega^{3}=0.

At
p=(x,\Pi)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{p}
can alternatively be obtained as the horizontal lift of
\Pi\subset T_{x}M
with respect to a metric in
[\mathsf{g}]
(and the result is independent of the choice of metric)
[
2
]
.
Let
\Gamma^{i}{}_{j}
be the Levi-Civita connection
1
-forms for (
2.2
) determined by the structure equations

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\theta^{i}+\Gamma^{i}{}_{j}\wedge\theta^{j}=0,\quad\Gamma_{ij}+\Gamma_{ji}=0,

where
\Gamma_{ij}=\mathsf{g}_{ik}\Gamma^{k}{}_{j}
, and
\Gamma^{i}{}_{jk}
are the connection coefficients defined via
\Gamma^{i}{}_{j}=\Gamma^{i}{}_{jk}\theta^{k}
.
Then
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathsf{ker}(\omega^{1},\omega^{2},\omega^{3})
, for
\omega^{1}
,
\omega^{2}
as in (
2.7
), and

(2.10)

\displaystyle\omega^{3}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{d}\xi-\Gamma^{2}{}_{4}+\xi(\Gamma^{3}{}_{3}+\Gamma^{2}{}_{2})-\xi^{2}\Gamma^{1}{}_{3}.

Adding
\omega^{4}=\theta^{1}
and
\omega^{5}=\theta^{4}
completes these to a coframing. Then a direct computation shows:

Lemma 2.3
.

We have

(2.11)

\mathsf{d}\omega^{3}\wedge\omega^{1}\wedge\omega^{2}\wedge\omega^{3}=\,(\Psi_{0}+4\Psi_{1}\xi+6\Psi_{2}\xi^{2}-4\Psi_{3}\xi^{3}+\Psi_{4}\xi^{4})\,\omega^{1}\wedge\omega^{2}\wedge\omega^{3}\wedge\omega^{4}\wedge\omega^{5},

where
the scalars
\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}
are the components of the SD Weyl tensor
\mathcal{W}^{+}
in the notation from
[
17
, p. 330]
.

It follows that
{\mathcal{D}}
is integrable if and only if
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}\equiv 0
, i.e.
[\mathsf{g}]
is ASD. Assuming that
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}\equiv 0
, one can pass to the local leaf space for the integrable distribution
\mathcal{D}
, which leads to the split-signature version of Penrose’s twistor construction
[
21
]
,
[
23
, Sec.7.3 & 7.4]
. In
[
2
]
, the opposite case was considered, i.e. when
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
is
non-vanishing
. It is shown that:

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be the twistor distribution on the total space
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
of the bundle of SD totally null
2
-planes. Then
{\mathcal{D}}
is a
(2,3,5)
-distribution on the open set of points in
{\mathbb{T}}^{+}
where

{\mathcal{W}}^{+}(\xi):=\Psi_{0}+4\Psi_{1}\xi+6\Psi_{2}\xi^{2}-4\Psi_{3}\xi^{3}+\Psi_{4}\xi^{4}\neq 0.

2.2.1.
An Example

Let us illustrate the construction from Section
2.2
on a special class of metrics that contains
conformally inequivalent
examples having
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric twistor distribution.
Let

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{d}x\mathsf{d}u+\mathsf{d}y\mathsf{d}v-\Theta_{xx}\mathsf{d}v^{2}-\Theta_{yy}\mathsf{d}u^{2}+2\Theta_{xy}\mathsf{d}u\mathsf{d}v

be the general Ricci-flat, SD, split-signature metric,
where the function
\Theta=\Theta(x,y,u,v)
satisfies
Plebański’s second heavenly equation

[
24
]
:

(2.13)

\displaystyle\Theta_{ux}+\Theta_{vy}+\Theta_{xx}\Theta_{yy}-\Theta^{2}_{xy}=0.

In
[
2
]
, the special case
\Theta=\Theta(x)
was considered with
\Theta^{(4)}=\Theta_{xxxx}\neq 0
. They showed that the associated twistor distribution
{\mathcal{D}}

has
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetry if and only if
\Theta
satisfies the ODE

(2.14)

\displaystyle 10(\Theta^{(4)})^{3}\Theta^{(8)}-70(\Theta^{(4)})^{2}\Theta^{(5)}\Theta^{(7)}-49(\Theta^{(4)})^{2}(\Theta^{(6)})^{2}+280\Theta^{(4)}(\Theta^{(5)})^{2}\Theta^{(6)}-175(\Theta^{(5)})^{4}=0.

This is equivalent to
\Theta^{\prime}
being a solution to the 7th order ODE studied by Dunajski–Sokolov
[
15
]
.

As a particular example,
consider the metric of the form
\mathsf{g}=2(\theta^{1}\theta^{2}+\theta^{3}\theta^{4})
, where

(2.15)

\displaystyle\theta^{1}=\mathsf{d}x,\quad\theta^{2}=\mathsf{d}u,\quad\theta^{3}=\mathsf{d}y-lx^{l-1}\mathsf{d}v,\quad\theta^{4}=\mathsf{d}v.

On the open subset with coordinates by
(x,y,u,v,\xi)
, we have
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathsf{ker}(\omega^{1},\omega^{2},\omega^{3})
, where

(2.16)

\displaystyle\omega^{1}=\mathsf{d}u+\xi\mathsf{d}v,\quad\omega^{2}=-\xi\mathsf{d}x+\mathsf{d}y-lx^{l-1}\mathsf{d}v,\quad\omega^{3}=\mathsf{d}\xi+(1-l)lx^{l-2}\mathsf{d}v.

(In
[
2
]
, a transformation is provided that brings (
2.16
) to the Monge form (
2.1
).) From (
2.11
),

(2.17)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}^{+}(\xi)=l(l-1)(l-2)x^{l-3}.

Thus,
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}\equiv 0
when
l\in\{0,1,2\}
(cases with
\Theta^{(4)}=0
), in which case
\mathsf{g}
is conformally flat and both SD and ASD twistor distributions are integrable. Otherwise,
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}
is of type
\mathsf{N}
(with quadruple root at
\xi=\infty
) and
{\mathcal{D}}
is
(2,3,5)
. From (
2.14
),
{\mathcal{D}}
is
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric if
l\in\{-1,\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2},3\}
.

For any
l
, the symmetry algebra of
[\mathsf{g}]
includes the 7-dimensional subalgebra

(2.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{y},\quad\partial_{u},\quad\partial_{v},\quad x\partial_{y}-v\partial_{u},\quad-2x^{l}\partial_{y}+y\partial_{u}-x\partial_{v},

\displaystyle x\partial_{x}+(l-1)y\partial_{y}+(l-2)u\partial_{u},\quad y\partial_{y}+2u\partial_{u}+v\partial_{v}.

If
l\in\{\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}\}
, this is the entire symmetry algebra. When
l\in\{-1,3\}
, it is augmented by two additional generators, e.g. when
l=3
, we also have

(2.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}+6xv\partial_{y}-3v^{2}\partial_{u},\quad-v\partial_{x}+(u-3xv^{2})\partial_{y}+v^{3}\partial_{u}.

Thus, there are conformally-inequivalent cases here. Referring to Theorem
1.1
, we have:

•

\mathsf{M9}
:
l=-1
and
l=3
(which are conformally equivalent);

•

\mathsf{M7}_{\mathsf{a}}
for
\mathsf{a}^{2}=\frac{4}{3}
:
l=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
l=\tfrac{3}{2}
(which are conformally equivalent).

2.3.
XXO structures

Motivated by the structure on the total space of the twistor bundle over a split-signature conformal structure, let us now introduce the general notion of an XXO-structure.

Definition 2.4
.

An XXO-structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
on a
5
-dimensional smooth manifold
N
consists of a rank
2
subbundle
{\mathcal{D}}\subset TN
and a line subbundle
\ell\subset TN
such that

(1)

\ell\cap{\mathcal{D}}=0
,

(2)

[\Gamma({\mathcal{D}}),\Gamma({\mathcal{D}})]\subset\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})
, where
{\mathcal{H}}:=\ell\oplus{\mathcal{D}}
,

(3)

the Lie bracket induces an isomorphism
\ell\otimes{\mathcal{D}}\cong TN/{\mathcal{H}}
.

The XXO-structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
is
integrable
if
{\mathcal{D}}
is (Frobenius) integrable. Otherwise, it is
non-integrable
.

Remark 2.5
.

We note that for a general XXO-structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
, the derived distribution
[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]
may not have constant rank.

If
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
is an integrable XXO-structure, then it is locally equivalent to a pair of 2nd order ODE. (This follows by combining Proposition 1.5 and Theorem 1.6 of
[
26
]
.)

Example 2.6
.

The geometry (up to point-equivalence) of a pair of 2nd order ODE,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\ddot{x}=F(t,x,y,\dot{x},\dot{y}),\quad\ddot{y}=G(t,x,y,\dot{x},\dot{y}),

is encoded by an integrable XXO-structure as follows. Consider the 1st jet space
N=J^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, which has dimension
5
, and standard local coordinates
(t,x,y,p,q)
for which the canonical Cartan distribution is
{\mathcal{C}}=\ker\langle dx-pdt,dy-qdt\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}+p\partial_{x}+q\partial_{y},\partial_{p},\partial_{q}\rangle\subset TN
. Define a splitting of
{\mathcal{C}}
into rank 1 and rank
2
subdistributions

(2.21)

\displaystyle\ell=\langle\partial_{t}+p\partial_{x}+q\partial_{y}+F\partial_{p}+G\partial_{q}\rangle,\quad{\mathcal{D}}=\langle\partial_{p},\partial_{q}\rangle,

where
F
and
G
are functions of
(t,x,y,p,q)
. Then
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
is an integrable XXO-structure on
N
.

Augmenting a
(2,3,5)
-distribution with additional structure yields non-integrable XXO-structures:

Example 2.7
.

Consider the
\mathrm{G}_{2}
-symmetric
(2,3,5)
-distribution

(2.22)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}=\left\langle D_{x}:=\partial_{x}+p\partial_{y}+q\partial_{p}+\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}\partial_{z},\,\partial_{q}\right\rangle

corresponding to
F=\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}
in (
2.1
). Then
[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]/{\mathcal{D}}
is represented by
\partial_{p}+q\partial_{z}
and we can define a non-integrable XXO-structure
(\ell,{\mathcal{D}})
via any choice of line field

(2.23)

\displaystyle\ell=\left\langle\partial_{p}+q\partial_{z}+AD_{x}+B\partial_{q}\right\rangle,

for functions
A,B
of
(x,y,p,q,z)
.

In each of these examples, the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
is the same, but different choices of line fields
\ell
may lead to inequivalent XXO-structures.

3.
Cartan geometric picture

Many features of the construction in Section
2.2
are best understood if one rephrases the construction in terms of the associated (parabolic) Cartan geometries.

3.1.
Parabolic geometries

Let
G/P
be a homogeneous space with corresponding Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
. A
Cartan geometry

({\mathcal{G}}\to M,\omega)
of type
({\mathfrak{g}},P)
is given by a right principal
P
-bundle
p:{\mathcal{G}}\to M
together with
a
Cartan connection

\omega\in\Omega^{1}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathfrak{g}})
. This satisfies:

(i)

\omega
defines an isomorphism
\omega_{u}:T_{u}{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}
for any
u\in{\mathcal{G}}
;

(ii)

\omega
is
P
-equivariant, i.e.
r_{g}^{*}\omega=\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\omega
for any
g\in P
;

(iii)

\omega
maps fundamental vector fields to their generators, i.e.
\omega(\zeta_{X})=X
for any
X\in{\mathfrak{p}}
.

In particular, the Cartan connection
\omega
provides an identification
{\mathcal{G}}\times_{P}{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}}\cong TM
.
A morphism between Cartan geometries
({\mathcal{G}}\to M,\omega)
and
({\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}\to M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
is a principal bundle morphism
\Phi:{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
such that
\Phi^{*}\omega^{\prime}=\omega
.

The
curvature
of
\omega
is the
2
-form
K\in\Omega^{2}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathfrak{g}})
defined as

(3.1)

\displaystyle K(X,Y)=\mathsf{d}\omega(X,Y)+[\omega(X),\omega(Y)]\quad\mbox{for}\quad X,Y\in\Gamma(T{\mathcal{G}}).

It is
P
-equivariant, horizontal, and can be equivalently encoded by the
curvature function

(3.2)

\displaystyle\kappa:{\mathcal{G}}\to\Lambda^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}})^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}},\quad\kappa(v,w):=K(\omega^{-1}(v),\omega^{-1}(w))\quad\mbox{for}\quad v,w\in{\mathfrak{g}}.

The curvature is a complete obstruction to local equivalence of
({\mathcal{G}}\to M,\omega)
with the homogeneous model
(G\to G/P,\omega_{G})
, where
\omega_{G}
denotes the left-invariant Maurer–Cartan form on
G
.

A
parabolic geometry
is a Cartan geometry of type
({\mathfrak{g}},P)
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple,
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
is a parabolic subalgebra, and
P\subset\text{Aut}({\mathfrak{g}})
is a Lie subgroup with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{p}}
. Parabolic subalgebras can be characterized by the property that the orthogonal complement
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
of
{\mathfrak{p}}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
with respect to the Killing form coincides with the nilradical of
{\mathfrak{p}}
, which will be denoted by
{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}
. It then follows that
{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}
is reductive and
{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}\cong({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}})^{*}
as
{\mathfrak{p}}
-modules. Defining
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}={\mathfrak{p}}_{+}
,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{i}=[{\mathfrak{g}}^{i-1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}]
for
i\geq 2
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{j+1}=({\mathfrak{g}}^{-j})^{\perp}
for
j\leq-1
, one obtains a (
depth

k
) filtration

(3.3)

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-k}\supset...\supset{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\supset...\supset{\mathfrak{g}}^{k},\quad[{\mathfrak{g}}^{i},{\mathfrak{g}}^{j}]\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{i+j},

which is
P
-invariant with respect to the adjoint action. The filtrand
{\mathfrak{g}}^{i}
is said to be of
homogeneity

\geq i
. There is a bijective correspondence between conjugacy classes of parabolic subalgebras and subsets of simple restricted roots, see
[
12
]
for details. In particular, parabolic subalgebras can be depicted by means of marked Satake diagrams. More precisely, a parabolic subalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}
is represented by crosses on the nodes of the Satake diagram corresponding to simple roots
\alpha_{i}
such that the root space
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\alpha_{i}}
is not contained in
{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Given the filtration (
3.3
), we can form its associated-graded Lie algebra
\mathrm{gr}({\mathfrak{g}})=\bigoplus_{i}\mathrm{gr}_{i}({\mathfrak{g}})
, where
\mathrm{gr}_{i}({\mathfrak{g}}):={\mathfrak{g}}^{i}/{\mathfrak{g}}^{i+1}
, and the bracket is induced. There is always a choice of grading element
\mathsf{Z}\in{\mathfrak{p}}
that lifts this grading on
\mathrm{gr}({\mathfrak{g}})
to a grading on
{\mathfrak{g}}
that is compatible with the filtration:

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-k}\oplus...\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\oplus...\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},\quad[{\mathfrak{g}}_{i},{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}]\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}_{i+j},\quad{\mathfrak{g}}^{i}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq i}.

Thus,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}={\mathfrak{p}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}={\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq 1}
.
Here,
\mathsf{Z}\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
(center of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
), and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
is the eigenspace of
{\rm ad}_{\mathsf{Z}}
for the eigenvalue
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, also called its
homogeneity
(which is compatible with
{\mathfrak{g}}^{i}
having homogeneity
\geq i
). Eigenvalues of
\mathsf{Z}
on any
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module will also be referred to as homogeneities.

A parabolic geometry is:

•

regular
if
\kappa
is of homogeneity
\geq 1
with respect to this filtration, i.e.
\kappa_{u}({\mathfrak{g}}^{i},{\mathfrak{g}}^{j})\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{i+j+1}
for any
u\in{\mathcal{G}}
,

•

normal
if
\partial^{*}\circ\kappa=0
, where
\partial^{*}
is the Lie algebra homology differential in the chain complex
(C_{\bullet}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+},{\mathfrak{g}}),\partial^{*})
, where
C_{k}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+},{\mathfrak{g}}):=\Lambda^{k}{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\cong_{\mathfrak{p}}\Lambda^{k}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}})^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.

The homology spaces
H_{k}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+},{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathrm{ker}(\partial^{*})/\mathrm{im}(\partial^{*})
are completely reducible
P
-modules (so
{\mathfrak{p}}_{+}
acts trivially on them), their
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module structure can be algorithmically computed using Kostant’s theorem
[
18
,
12
]
, and this can be explicitly identified with so-called
harmonic elements
of
\Lambda^{2}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
. The projection of the curvature
\kappa
of a regular normal parabolic geometry to
\mathrm{ker}(\partial^{*})/\mathrm{im}(\partial^{*})=H_{2}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+},{\mathfrak{g}})
is called the
harmonic curvature

\kappa_{H}
. (By regularity, it is valued in
H_{2}({\mathfrak{p}}_{+},{\mathfrak{g}})^{1}
, i.e. the positive homogeneity part.) The harmonic curvature is a simpler object than the full curvature, but it determines the full curvature via a differential operator. In particular, vanishing of
\kappa_{H}
on an open neighbourhood implies vanishing of
\kappa
there
[
12
, Theorem 3.1.12]
.

3.2.
Categorical equivalences

There is a fundamental theorem establishing an equivalence of categories between regular normal parabolic geometries of type
({\mathfrak{g}},P)
and certain underlying geometric structures, see
[
12
, Theorem 3.1.14]
. In Table
2
, we specify the structure and corresponding model data
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{p}})
, for which we define
P\subset\text{Aut}({\mathfrak{g}})
as the subgroup preserving the associated filtration (
3.3
). For these three cases, the underlying structure corresponds to the
P
-submodule
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}/{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}}
via the associated bundle construction
{\mathcal{G}}\times_{P}{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Table 2.
Model data for our structures of interest

We remark that the model homogeneous space in the 4D split-conformal case is the Grassmannian of 2-planes in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, while in the XXO case it is the flag manifold
{\mathbb{F}}_{1,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})
of lines incident on 2-planes. For parabolic subgroups, we will use notations
P,\widetilde{P},\widetilde{Q}
respective to
{\mathfrak{p}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}
defined in Table
2
.

Recall that
\mathrm{SL}(4,{\mathbb{R}})\cong\mathrm{Spin}(3,3)
, and we can take
\widetilde{Q}\subset\mathrm{SL}(4,{\mathbb{R}})
as the stabilizer of the 2-plane
\left\langle e_{1},e_{2}\right\rangle\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, i.e.

(3.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{pmatrix}:\ A,B\in\mathrm{GL}(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\ \mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1\right\}.

For this choice of groups
\widetilde{Q}\subset\mathrm{SL}(4,{\mathbb{R}})
, the general theory implies that there an equivalence of categories between 4D split-conformal
spin
structures and regular normal parabolic geometries of type
(\mathfrak{sl}(4,{\mathbb{R}}),\widetilde{Q})
. In particular,
\pm\mathrm{id}_{4}
act trivially on
\mathrm{SL}(4,{\mathbb{R}})/\widetilde{Q}
.

To see the conformal structure on the quotient
\mathfrak{sl}(4,{\mathbb{R}})/\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}
, identify this with the space of
2\times 2
matrices
\mathrm{M}_{2}{\mathbb{R}}
, embedded as the bottom-left
2\times 2
block in
\mathfrak{sl}(4,{\mathbb{R}})
. The
\widetilde{Q}
-action (induced by the adjoint action) on this space is given by
X\mapsto BXA^{-1}
(the
C
-block acts trivially on the quotient). Now the determinant defines a scalar product of signature
(2,2)
on
\mathrm{M}_{2}{\mathbb{R}}
, which is conformally preserved by the action, since
\mathrm{det}(BXA^{-1})=(\mathrm{det}(B))^{2}\mathrm{det}(X)
.

Table 3.
Harmonic curvature for our structures of interest

The component
{\mathcal{I}}
is given by
(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{pr}_{\ell}([x,y])
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{\ell}:{\mathcal{H}}\to\ell
is the projection of
{\mathcal{H}}=\ell\oplus{\mathcal{D}}
along
\ell
. It is precisely the obstruction to integrability of the rank
2
distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
.

For pairs of 2nd order ODEs, i.e. integrable XXO-structures, the components
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}
specialize to the
Fels curvature
and
Fels torsion
respectively
[
16
]
. In Appendix
A
, we give a formula for
{\mathcal{T}}
for general XXO-structures – see (
A.9
).

In Section
5.4
, we will need specific information about the harmonic curvature module
H_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{+},\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
in the 4D split-conformal setting that we summarize here. Recall the model data
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(4,{\mathbb{R}}),\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}})
from Section
3.2
, which induces a decreasing filtration
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\supset\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}\supset\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}
. As a module for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
,
H_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{+},\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
is identified with so-called harmonic elements in
\Lambda^{2}\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}^{*}\otimes\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. By Kostant’s theorem
[
18
]
, this module decomposes into two
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-irreps.

Explicitly, let
E_{ij}
be the
4\times 4
matrix with a 1 in the
(i,j)
-position, and 0 elsewhere. Also define
H_{ij}:=E_{ii}-E_{jj}
. The trace form
T(A,B)=\mathrm{tr}(AB)
on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
identifies
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}})^{*}\cong_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}
, in particular

(3.6)

\displaystyle(E_{13},E_{14},E_{23},E_{24})\leftrightarrow(E_{31}^{*},E_{41}^{*},E_{32}^{*},E_{42}^{*}),

Kostant’s theorem yields lowest weight vectors
\psi_{0},\phi_{0}
for the two
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-irreps, on which the raising operators
E_{12}
and
E_{34}
can be applied – see Table
4
. The second column gives the abstract structure of these modules when viewed as binary quartics for the respective
\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-actions.
